| S4.8 | The hash **length is adaptive** in the issue count. Lengths are tried in ascending order from `min_hash_length` 3 through `max_hash_length` 8. For a candidate length the space is 36 raised to that length, evaluated as a 64-bit binary float, and the birthday-bound collision estimate is one minus the exponential of the negated quantity (count × count) ÷ (2 × space), also in 64-bit binary float. The first length whose estimate is **strictly less than** `max_collision_prob` 0.25 is chosen. When no length in 3..=8 satisfies it, the result is 8. Equivalently, over exact integers the predicate is `count × count < 0.575364144903562 × 36^length`, where the constant is the negated natural logarithm of 0.75 | count 0 → 3; count 8 → 3; count 163 → 3; count 164 → 4 | `src/util/id.rs:103` | create_min (count 0), create_full_json (count 8) |
| S4.9 | **The adaptive-length table.** Evaluating S4.8 over every 64-bit-float boundary gives exactly these issue-count bands. The corpus never leaves the first band, so bands 4 and up are computed, not captured — OQ-006 remains open on them and the proposed boundary cases are listed under OQ proposals. [inference] for every row except the first Measured (2026-09-22, OQ-006): the first two steps are captured on fixtures the original built by creating issues one at a time (`idlen_163`, `idlen_164`, `idlen_983`, `idlen_984` scenarios): a create over 163 records mints a 3-character hash (`proj-t8o`), over 164 a 4-character one (`proj-xt8o`), over 983 still 4, over 984 a 5-character one (`proj-7xt8o`); the stores themselves hold 164 three-character ids, then four-character ones. The bands from 5898 up remain computed, not captured. | count `0`–`163` → length **3**; `164`–`983` → **4**; `984`–`5898` → **5**; `5899`–`35389` → **6**; `35390`–`212339` → **7**; `212340` and above → **8** (length is never 9 or more) | `src/util/id.rs:103` | create_min, create_full_json; (cases to add: `edge_idlen_163`, `edge_idlen_164` — `["@fx=n163","create","Boundary"]` / `["@fx=n164","create","Boundary"]`), edge_idlen_163, edge_idlen_164, edge_idlen_983, edge_idlen_984 |
| S4.10 | The count the predicate sees in `--no-db` is the number of **records in the store**, unfiltered: every record loaded from `.beads/issues.jsonl` counts, including `closed`, `deferred`, `draft` and **`tombstone`** records, because `--no-db` rebuilds an in-memory issue table from the whole file and the count is a plain row count over that table with no status predicate. Ephemeral issues are never written to the file, so they never count on a later invocation. The count is read **once**, before the id loop, and does not change across collision retries within one invocation. Unobservable in the corpus because every fixture is far below 164; [inference] Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-028): tombstones count: 163 live records and one tombstone mint a four-character id (`proj-lff7`). | fixture `basic` (8 records) → count 8 → length 3; fixture `empty` (0 records) → count 0 → length 3; after `delete_basic` the tombstoned `proj-5u2` is still a record and still counts | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:12263`, `src/cli/commands/create.rs:452` | create_min, create_full_json, delete_basic, edge_idlen_tombstones |
| S4.11 | **Collision ladder.** At the chosen length, nonces `0,1,2,…,9` are tried in order; the first candidate the store does not already hold is the answer. If all ten collide and the length is below 8, the length increases by one and the ten nonces are tried again at the new length. The nonce restarts at 0 at every length | three `create` calls with identical title, creator and instant in one workspace: nonce 0 → `proj-86w` (free), nonce 0 → `proj-86w` (taken) then nonce 1 → `proj-e1f`, nonce 0 and 1 taken then nonce 2 → `proj-fa9`. All three **✓recomputed** from the same seed differing only in the nonce part | `src/util/id.rs:230` | scn_dup_title |
| S4.12 | **Fallback rung.** When length 8 has also exhausted nonces 0..9, the length is abandoned and a fixed 12-character hash is used with nonces `0,1,2,…,1999` in order (the same seed rule, the same digest rule, `length` 12). The first free candidate wins. If all 2000 collide, one final candidate is built with nonce `2000` at length 12; it is returned when free, and when it too is taken the run fails with `Issue ID collision: <id>` on stderr, code `ID_COLLISION`, exit **3**. Unreachable in the corpus | nonce 1999 candidate taken and nonce 2000 candidate taken → `Error: Issue ID collision: proj-<12 chars>` | `src/util/id.rs:241`, `src/util/id.rs:12`, `src/error/structured.rs:245` | (case to add: `error_id_collision` — needs a fixture holding every candidate) |
| S4.13 | The existence test used by the ladder is "a record with exactly this id is present in the store", with no status filter, so a **tombstone occupies its id** and a colliding candidate is skipped. [inference]: no captured case forces a collision against a tombstone Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-029): a tombstone holding the candidate blocks it; the next candidate is taken (`proj-fhn`). | store holds tombstone `proj-5u2` → a candidate `proj-5u2` is treated as taken | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:12093` | delete_basic; (case to add: `edge_id_collides_with_tombstone`), edge_id_collides_with_tombstone |
| S4.14 | On top of the ladder, `create` retries the whole id generation up to **10 further times** when the store itself reports a collision at insert time, sleeping 10, 20, … milliseconds between attempts; each retry re-reads the clock, so the seed's timestamp part changes. In `--no-db` the store is a fresh private in-memory table, so this path is never taken and the sleep never happens | — | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:576` | create_min |
| S4.15 | `create` feeds the id seed the **raw** title exactly as given on the command line (no trim, no case change) and the raw `-d`/`--description`/`--description-file` value, and the creator is the resolved actor (`tester` in the pinned environment, or the `--actor` value). `--title` and the positional title are the same input to the seed | `create "First issue"` → title part `11:First issue`; `create "By actor" --actor robot-7` → creator part `7:robot-7` → `proj-k6b` **✓recomputed** | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:406`, `src/cli/commands/create.rs:458` | create_min, create_title_flag, actor_flag_create, create_silent |
| S4.16 | `q` (quick capture) feeds a **different** seed: the title is the positional words joined with a single space and then trimmed, the description part is **empty** and the creator part is **empty** even when an actor is configured. Everything else (SHA-256, byte order, base36, adaptive length, ladder) is identical | `q "Quick one"` → seed `9:Quick one0:0:19:17673230450000000001:0` → `proj-asw` **✓recomputed**; `q "Quick urgent" -p 0` → `proj-tkn` **✓recomputed** | `src/cli/commands/q.rs:47`, `src/cli/commands/q.rs:120` | q_basic, q_priority |
| S4.17 | `--dry-run` still generates the id through the full ladder and prints it; nothing is written | `create "Not written" --dry-run` → `Dry run: would create issue proj-8h9` → `proj-8h9` **✓recomputed** | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:540` | create_dry_run, create_dry_run_json |
| S4.18 | `--ephemeral` does not change id generation; the id is generated and reported, and only the write-back differs | `create "Scratch" --ephemeral` → `proj-kr0` **✓recomputed** | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:458` | create_ephemeral |

### Optional slug form (`--slug`)

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.19 | With a `--slug` value that is non-empty after trimming, the id becomes `<prefix>-<slug>-<hash>`: prefix, `-`, the normalized slug, `-`, the hash portion. The seed, digest and base36 rules are unchanged; only the assembled string differs | prefix `proj`, slug `My Thing!`, hash `fyw` → `proj-my-thing-fyw` | `src/util/id.rs:182` | (case to add: `create_slug` — `["create","Slugged","--slug","My Thing!","--json"]`) |
| S4.20 | **Slug normalization.** Characters are scanned in order. An ASCII letter is lowercased and kept; an ASCII digit is kept; every other character contributes a single `-`, and consecutive such characters contribute only one `-`. A non-alphanumeric run at the very start contributes nothing (a `-` is only appended once output is non-empty), so the result never starts with `-`. Trailing `-` characters are then removed. If the result exceeds **48** characters it is truncated to 48 and trailing `-` characters are removed again. Non-ASCII characters count as "other" and collapse to `-` | `My Thing!` → `my-thing`; `  Survey — my  thing  ` → `survey-my-thing`; `!!!` → empty | `src/util/id.rs:640`, `src/util/id.rs:628` | (case to add: `create_slug_unicode`) |
| S4.21 | **Prefix budget.** After S4.20 the slug is further truncated so that its length does not exceed `64 − prefix length − 1`, with trailing `-` characters removed again. When the prefix is 63 characters or longer the budget is zero or underflows and the slug becomes empty | prefix `proj` (4) → budget 59; prefix of 64 characters → budget underflows → slug empty | `src/util/id.rs:668` | (case to add: `create_slug_long_prefix`) |
| S4.22 | When the slug normalizes to the empty string, generation falls back to the plain hash-only path of S4.7–S4.12 with the same seed | `--slug "!!!"` → id has no slug segment | `src/util/id.rs:157` | (case to add: `create_slug_empty`) |
| S4.23 | **Slug collision ladder.** At the adaptive length, nonces 0..9 are tried; if all collide and the length is below 8 the length increases by one and the ten nonces repeat. When length 8 is exhausted the slug is **dropped entirely** and generation restarts on the hash-only path (S4.11–S4.12) rather than widening the slug segment | all slug candidates through length 8 taken → id without a slug segment | `src/util/id.rs:169` | (case to add: `create_slug_saturated`) |

### Child ids

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.24 | With `--parent`, the id is **not** hashed. It is the resolved parent id, then `.` (U+002E), then a decimal child number: `<parent>.<n>`. The number has no padding and no sign | parent `proj-uly`, number 1 → `proj-uly.1`; parent `proj-uly.1`, number 1 → `proj-uly.1.1` | `src/util/id.rs:352`, `src/cli/commands/create.rs:604` | scn_child_ids, create_with_parent |
| S4.25 | **How `n` is chosen in `--no-db`.** No counter survives between invocations (the counter table is not part of the JSONL). Each invocation rebuilds the counter table from the loaded records: every record whose id parses as non-root and whose **direct parent id is also present** contributes its last path segment to that parent's maximum. `n` is that maximum plus 1, and 1 when the parent has no child yet. A tombstoned child still contributes its number, so numbers are not reused after a delete. [inference] on the tombstone half — no captured case deletes a child Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-030): a tombstoned child still counts: after `delete proj-uly.2` the next child is `proj-uly.3` (`scn_child_after_delete`). | parent `proj-uly` with no children → `proj-uly.1`; then `proj-uly.2`; parent `proj-uly.1` with no children → `proj-uly.1.1` (the grandchild raises `proj-uly.1`'s maximum, not `proj-uly`'s, so the next child of `proj-uly` stays `proj-uly.3`) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:14398`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:10907`, `src/sync/mod.rs:14380` | scn_child_ids, scn_child_after_delete |
| S4.26 | If the computed child id already exists, the number is incremented until a free one is found, at most **100** increments past the computed number; beyond that the run fails with `Validation failed: parent: could not find available child ID` on stderr, exit **4**. Unreachable in the corpus | — | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:612` | (case to add: `error_child_id_exhausted`) |
| S4.27 | Child nesting has **no explicit depth limit**. Depth is bounded only by the whole-id length cap of S4.35 (105 bytes) and by each segment fitting an unsigned 32-bit integer. `q --parent` uses the identical child-number rule as S4.25–S4.26 | `proj-uly.1.1` is accepted at depth 2 | `src/util/id.rs:384`, `src/cli/commands/q.rs:87` | scn_child_ids |

### Id parsing, validation and prefix normalization

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.28 | An id is split at its **last** `-`: everything before it is the prefix, everything after it is the remainder. A missing `-`, an empty prefix, or an empty remainder makes the id invalid. This lets a prefix itself contain `-` | `proj-mta` → prefix `proj`, remainder `mta`; `my-team-abc` → prefix `my-team`, remainder `abc`; `abc` → invalid; `-abc` → invalid; `abc-` → invalid | `src/util/id.rs:356`, `src/util/id.rs:360` | show_plain, error_show_missing |
| S4.29 | The prefix must be non-empty, at most **64** characters, and made only of ASCII lowercase letters, ASCII digits, `_`, `-`, `.`, `:` and `#`. Any other character (including an ASCII uppercase letter) makes the id invalid | `proj` → valid; `PROJ-mta` → invalid; `a.b:c#d_e-f` → valid prefix | `src/util/id.rs:485`, `src/util/id.rs:9` | show_plain |
| S4.30 | The remainder is split on `.`; the first segment is the hash. The hash must be non-empty, at most **40** characters, and made only of ASCII lowercase letters and ASCII digits | `mta` → hash `mta`; `MTA` → invalid; a 41-character hash → invalid | `src/util/id.rs:502`, `src/util/id.rs:10` | show_plain |
| S4.31 | Every segment after the hash must be non-empty, made only of ASCII digits, and must parse as an unsigned 32-bit integer (0 through 4294967295). A segment that is empty, signed, non-digit or out of range makes the id invalid. Leading zeros are accepted by the parse and are preserved in the input string | `proj-uly.1.2` → child path 1, 2; `proj-uly.` → invalid; `proj-uly.-1` → invalid; `proj-uly.4294967296` → invalid | `src/util/id.rs:517` | scn_child_ids |
| S4.32 | An id is a child id when the part after the prefix separator contains a `.`; when the id has no prefix separator at all, the test falls back to "the whole string contains a `.`". Depth is the count of `.` in that same part, so a root id has depth 0 | `proj-uly.1` → child, depth 1; `proj-uly.1.1` → depth 2; `proj-mta` → depth 0 | `src/util/id.rs:372`, `src/util/id.rs:384` | scn_child_ids |
| S4.33 | The parent of a parsed id is the id with its last child segment removed: with one segment it is `<prefix>-<hash>`, with more it is `<prefix>-<hash>` followed by the remaining segments each preceded by `.`. A root id has no parent. An id is a child of another when it starts with that other id and the next character is `.` | `proj-uly.1.1` → parent `proj-uly.1`; `proj-uly.1` → parent `proj-uly`; `proj-uly` → no parent | `src/util/id.rs:428`, `src/util/id.rs:457` | scn_child_ids |
| S4.34 | Prefix validation against a configured prefix succeeds when the parsed prefix equals the expected prefix or appears in the allowed list; otherwise the run fails with `Prefix mismatch: expected '<expected>', found '<found>'`, code `PREFIX_MISMATCH`, exit **4** | expected `proj`, id `other-abc` → `Prefix mismatch: expected 'proj', found 'other'` | `src/util/id.rs:547`, `src/error/mod.rs:116` | (case to add: `error_prefix_mismatch`) |
| S4.35 | Record-level id validation additionally rejects an id that is empty after trimming, an id longer than **105** bytes (64 + 1 + 40), and an id that fails S4.28–S4.31, with the reasons `id: cannot be empty`, `id: exceeds 105 characters` and `id: invalid format (expected prefix-hash)` | a 106-byte id → `id: exceeds 105 characters` | `src/validation/mod.rs:84`, `src/util/id.rs:11` | (case to add: `error_jsonl_id_too_long`) |
| S4.36 | Id normalization for lookup is a **full Unicode lowercase** of the input (not ASCII-only), applied after trimming. A non-ASCII uppercase character therefore folds by Unicode rules and may change byte length Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-118): `show PROJ-MTÄ` is `Issue not found: PROJ-MTÄ`, quoting the id as typed. | `PROJ-MTA` → `proj-mta`; `İ` folds to two code points | `src/util/id.rs:566` | (case to add: `show_uppercase_id`), error_show_nonascii_id |
| S4.37 | **Prefix normalization** of an inferred prefix: trim, then per character lowercase it by ASCII rules and keep it only when it is an ASCII lowercase letter, ASCII digit, `_`, `-`, `.`, `:` or `#`; take at most the first 64 kept characters; then strip every trailing `_`, `-`, `.`, `:` and `#`. When nothing remains the inferred prefix is `br` | `  My_Project!  ` → `my_project`; `///` → `br`; `proj` → `proj` | `src/util/id.rs:570`, `src/util/id.rs:596` | where_plain |
| S4.38 | An **explicitly configured** prefix runs the same normalization but, when nothing remains, fails with `issue_prefix "<raw value>" contains no usable ASCII letters, digits, or separators` (the raw value rendered with Rust debug quoting), rather than defaulting to `br` | configured `///` → that message | `src/util/id.rs:615` | (case to add: `error_config_prefix_unusable`) |
| S4.39 | **Prefix abbreviation** (used when the prefix is derived from the directory name): normalize per S4.37; a result of 6 characters or fewer is used unchanged. Otherwise the result is split on every non-alphanumeric character, empty pieces dropped; when two or more pieces remain, the first character of each is concatenated and used when that concatenation is longer than one character. Failing that, the first three alphanumeric characters are used, and when even that is empty the normalized value is used | `proj` (4) → `proj`; `my_long_project` → `mlp`; `verylongword` → `ver` | `src/util/id.rs:692` | where_plain |

### Partial-id resolution

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.40 | The user-supplied id is first trimmed of leading and trailing whitespace. An input that is empty after trimming fails with `Invalid issue ID format: ` (the id part is the empty string), code `INVALID_ID`, exit **3** | `"  "` → that error | `src/util/id.rs:853` | (case to add: `error_show_blank_id`) |
| S4.41 | **Step 1, exact match (highest precedence).** The trimmed input is lowercased per S4.36 and looked up as a complete id. A hit ends resolution immediately, before any prefix repair or substring search | `proj-mta` → `proj-mta`; `PROJ-MTA` → `proj-mta` | `src/util/id.rs:862` | show_plain, show_two |
| S4.42 | **Step 2, missing prefix.** Only when the lowercased input contains **no** `-` at all, the configured default prefix and `-` are prepended and that is looked up as a complete id. A hit ends resolution | `mta` with default prefix `proj` → `proj-mta` | `src/util/id.rs:871` | show_partial_hash |
| S4.43 | **Step 3, substring match on the hash portion.** The lowercased input is split per S4.28. When it splits, the part before the last `-` is remembered as a required prefix and the part after it is the search pattern; when it does not split, the whole input is the pattern and no prefix is required. An empty pattern skips this step. Every stored id is tested: the candidate's own remainder is taken, its base hash is the text before the candidate's first `.`, and the candidate qualifies when its base hash **contains** the pattern's base part as a substring. When the pattern itself contains a `.`, the candidate must additionally have a child path that equals the pattern's part after the first `.` **exactly**. Candidates are then filtered to those starting with the required prefix followed by `-`, when a prefix was remembered | `m` → `proj-7vm`, `proj-mkh`, `proj-mta` (every id whose base hash contains `m`); `mta` after step 2 misses would match `proj-mta`; `other-mta` → candidates filtered to those starting `other-` → none | `src/util/id.rs:883`, `src/util/id.rs:1060` | show_partial_ambiguous, error_show_ambiguous_json, error_update_ambiguous |
| S4.44 | The candidate list is produced in **ascending id order** by the store (a plain byte-order sort of the id text), and the harness additionally sorts the rendered list, so the captured order is ascending in both readings (see DISC-005 and the S10 handover) | store ids sorted → `proj-7vm`, `proj-mkh`, `proj-mta` (`7` = 0x37 sorts before `m` = 0x6D) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:12302`, `scripts/ws_inner.py:105` | show_partial_ambiguous, error_show_ambiguous_json |
| S4.45 | Exactly one candidate resolves to that id. Zero candidates falls through to step 4 | `mta` → `proj-mta` | `src/util/id.rs:897` | show_partial_hash |
| S4.46 | Two or more candidates fail with `Ambiguous ID '<input>': matches ["<id>", "<id>", …]` — the **untrimmed-case original input** in single quotes, then the candidate list rendered with Rust debug formatting: `[`, each id in double quotes, separated by `, `, then `]`. The hint is `Provide more characters of the ID`. Code `AMBIGUOUS_ID`, exit **3**, retryable true. Plain mode writes `Error: <message>` then `Hint: <hint>` to **stderr** and nothing to stdout; JSON mode writes the pretty-printed error envelope to **stdout** with context fields `partial_id` and `matches` | `show m` → stderr `Error: Ambiguous ID 'm': matches ["proj-7vm", "proj-mkh", "proj-mta"]` and `Hint: Provide more characters of the ID`, exit 3 | `src/error/mod.rs:70`, `src/error/structured.rs:671` | show_partial_ambiguous, error_show_ambiguous_json, error_update_ambiguous |
| S4.47 | **Step 4, no match.** The run fails with `Issue not found: <input>` using the trimmed original input (not the lowercased or prefixed form), hint `Run 'br list' to see available issues.`, code `ISSUE_NOT_FOUND`, exit **3**, retryable false, JSON context field `searched_id` | `show proj-zzz` → stderr `Error: Issue not found: proj-zzz`, exit 3 | `src/util/id.rs:919`, `src/error/structured.rs:668` | error_show_missing, error_show_missing_json, error_update_missing, error_close_missing, error_reopen_missing, error_delete_missing, error_comments_add_missing, error_create_missing_parent |
| S4.48 | When a command takes several ids, they are resolved **left to right** and the first failure ends the run; ids to its right are never resolved and nothing is mutated Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-119): `show proj-mta proj-zzz` prints nothing of `proj-mta` (`show_two_second_missing`). | `show proj-mta proj-5u2` → both; `update m -p 1` → ambiguity error at the first id | `src/util/id.rs:1017` | show_two, close_two, update_two_ids, error_update_ambiguous, show_two_second_missing |
| S4.49 | A dependency argument beginning with `external:` bypasses resolution entirely and is stored verbatim. Otherwise, when resolution fails, `create --deps` and `dep add` retry the argument as an **exact title** match against the store: no title match re-raises the resolution error, one match uses that id, and several matches fail with `Validation failed: deps: dependency title '<input>' matches multiple issues: <id>, <id>` (comma-space joined), exit **4** | `dep add proj-mta external:other-1` → edge to `external:other-1` | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:661` | dep_add_external, create_with_deps |

### Content hash

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.50 | The content hash is SHA-256 over a fixed sequence of length-prefixed fields, each written as the field's byte length in an **unsigned 64-bit little-endian** 8-byte prefix followed by the field's raw UTF-8 bytes. The order is: title; description; design; acceptance criteria; notes; status text; priority rendered as a decimal integer; issue-type text; assignee; owner; created-by; external ref; source system; the literal `pinned` when pinned and empty otherwise; the literal `template` when a template and empty otherwise. Then seventeen reserved placeholder fields in this order: empty, empty, empty, empty, `0`, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty, empty (the fifth is the literal `0`; the second is the empty-valued `crystallizes` flag). Finally, when prerequisites are present and non-empty, two more fields: the literal `prerequisites` then the prerequisites text. An absent optional is the empty string. The digest is rendered as 64 lowercase hexadecimal characters | title `Test Issue`, description `A test description`, status `open`, priority `2`, type `task`, everything else absent → a 64-character lowercase hex string | `src/util/hash.rs:101`, `src/util/hash.rs:198` | (case to add: none is possible — the value reaches no output and no decision; see S4.51) |
| S4.51 | **The content hash is not observable in `--no-db`.** The field is skipped by the record's JSON representation, so it appears in no `--json` payload and in no line of `.beads/issues.jsonl`; and no in-scope command's decision reads it (the only readers are the SQLite export-hash tracking and the sync witness, both excluded from scope by PLAN §3). S4.50 is recorded for completeness and the port reproduces no byte from it. Confirmed against every golden: the string `content_hash` occurs in none of them | every captured stdout and every captured `.beads/issues.jsonl` dump → no `content_hash` key | `src/model/mod.rs:467`, `src/util/hash.rs:77` | create_min, list_json, edge_precision_rewrite (all show the key absent) |

### Time: producing, re-serializing and parsing timestamps

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.52 | A mutating command reads the wall clock **once per attempt** and uses that single instant for every timestamp it writes in that attempt: `created_at`, `updated_at`, a dependency's `created_at`, a comment's `created_at`, `deleted_at`, and `closed_at` when the new status is closed. In the pinned sandbox that instant is fixed, so all of them are byte-identical within one invocation | `create "First issue"` at the pinned instant → `created_at` and `updated_at` both `2026-01-02T03:04:05Z`; `create "Child A" --parent proj-uly` → the record and its `parent-child` edge share `2026-02-04T09:00:00Z` | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:456`, `src/cli/commands/create.rs:474` | create_min, scn_child_ids, comments_add |
| S4.53 | `closed_at` is set to that instant exactly when the status becomes `closed`, and `deleted_at` exactly when the status becomes `tombstone`. A record that is neither closed nor a tombstone carrying `closed_at` is a validation failure (`closed_at: only closed or tombstone issues may set closed_at`), and a closed record without it fails with `closed_at: closed issues must set closed_at` | `delete proj-5u2` → `deleted_at` and `updated_at` both `2026-01-02T03:04:05Z` | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:490`, `src/validation/mod.rs:130` | close_basic, delete_basic, update_status_closed |
| S4.54 | **Re-serialization.** Every timestamp written to `.beads/issues.jsonl` and to a `--json` record is RFC 3339 in **UTC with the literal `Z`**, never a numeric offset, and with a fraction whose digit count is 0, 3, 6 or 9 — the smallest of those that represents the value exactly. A whole second prints no fraction and no decimal point | `2026-01-01T10:00:05Z` → `2026-01-01T10:00:05Z`; `…05.100Z` → `…05.100Z`; `…05.1Z` → `…05.100Z` (widened to 3); `…05.000000001Z` → `…05.000000001Z` (9 digits) | `src/model/mod.rs:519` | edge_precision_list_json, edge_precision_show_json, edge_precision_rewrite, create_fraction_json, create_fraction_eighth, update_fraction, close_fraction_json |
| S4.55 | A stored timestamp with a **numeric offset** is converted to the same instant in UTC and re-serialized per S4.54, so the offset never survives a rewrite | `2026-01-01T12:00:04+02:00` → `2026-01-01T10:00:04Z` | `src/util/time.rs:146` | edge_precision_rewrite, edge_precision_ready |
| S4.56 | **Flexible timestamp parsing** (used by `--due`, `--defer`, `defer --until`) trims the input and tries the forms in this fixed order, taking the first that succeeds: (1) RFC 3339; (2) the bare date `YYYY-MM-DD`; (3) a signed relative duration; (4) a keyword. A value matching none of them is a validation failure | — | `src/util/time.rs:35` | defer_until_date, defer_relative, error_defer_bad_until, create_full_json |
| S4.57 | **Form 1, RFC 3339.** Any RFC 3339 spelling is accepted, including `Z` and a numeric offset, with any fraction length, and is converted to UTC. In addition, an input whose last nine bytes are a sign, two digits, `:`, two digits, `:`, then exactly `00` is retried with those trailing three bytes removed — so a zero **offset-seconds** spelling parses. Non-zero offset seconds are rejected Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-099): on load a space for `T`, a lower-case `t` and `z`, and an offset are accepted and written back as `…T10:00:00Z`. | `2025-01-15T12:00:00Z` → that instant; `2025-01-15T12:00:00+00:00` → the same instant; `2025-01-15T12:00:00+00:00:00` → the same instant; `2025-01-15T12:00:00+00:00:01` → rejected; `1969-12-31T23:59:59.123456789Z` → a pre-epoch instant, nanoseconds preserved | `src/util/time.rs:145`, `src/util/time.rs:156` | (case to add: `defer_until_rfc3339`), edge_time_spellings, edge_time_spellings_ok |
| S4.58 | **Form 2, bare date.** `YYYY-MM-DD` with exactly that layout becomes **09:00:00 local time** on that date, converted to UTC. The pinned sandbox sets `TZ=UTC`, so the captured result is 09:00:00Z | `--until 2027-06-01` → `2027-06-01T09:00:00+00:00` in the command's JSON payload and `2027-06-01T09:00:00Z` in the store; `--due 2026-03-01` → `due_at` `2026-03-01T09:00:00Z` | `src/util/time.rs:44` | defer_until_date, create_full_json |
| S4.59 | **Form 3, relative duration.** The value starts with `+` or `-`; its **last character** is the unit and everything before that last character — the sign included — is parsed as a signed 64-bit integer. The units are `m` minutes, `h` hours, `d` days (86400 seconds), `w` weeks (7 days). The duration is added to the current instant. A last character that is none of those four fails with `<field>: invalid unit (use m, h, d, w)`. An amount that overflows a signed 64-bit integer, a unit-times-amount product that overflows, or an addition that leaves the representable range fails with `<field>: relative duration is out of supported range`. A leading `+`/`-` whose remaining amount does not parse as an integer is **not** an error here: it falls through to form 4 | `+1h` at the pinned instant → `2026-01-02T04:04:05+00:00`; `-7d` → seven days earlier; `+30m`; `+1w`; `+9999999999999999999d` → out-of-range message | `src/util/time.rs:183` | defer_relative |
| S4.60 | **Form 4, keywords.** The trimmed input is lowercased and matched exactly against `today` → 17:00:00 local today; `yesterday` → 09:00:00 local yesterday; `tomorrow` → 09:00:00 local tomorrow; `next-week` and `nextweek` → 09:00:00 local seven days from today. Each is converted to UTC | `tomorrow` at the pinned instant with `TZ=UTC` → `2026-01-03T09:00:00Z` | `src/util/time.rs:69` | (case to add: `defer_until_tomorrow`) |
| S4.61 | **Local-time resolution.** A local wall time that is ambiguous (a fall-back repeated hour) resolves to the **earlier** UTC instant. A local wall time that does not exist (a spring-forward gap) is retried with one hour added; when that also does not exist the value fails with `<field>: invalid local time around DST transition`. With `TZ=UTC` neither path is reachable in the corpus | — | `src/util/time.rs:294` | (case to add: `defer_until_dst_gap` — needs a non-UTC `TZ`) |
| S4.62 | A value matching no form fails with the verbatim reason `invalid time format (try: +1h, -7d, tomorrow, next-week, or 2025-01-15)`. The field name is `defer_until` for `defer --until` and `date` for `create --due` and `create --defer`. Plain mode writes `Error: Validation failed: <field>: <reason>` to **stderr**, nothing to stdout, exit **4** | `defer proj-mta --until not-a-date` → stderr `Error: Validation failed: defer_until: invalid time format (try: +1h, -7d, tomorrow, next-week, or 2025-01-15)`, exit 4 | `src/util/time.rs:93`, `src/cli/commands/defer.rs:254`, `src/cli/commands/create.rs:1497` | error_defer_bad_until; (case to add: `error_create_bad_due`) |
| S4.63 | An empty or whitespace-only `--due`/`--defer` value on `create` is treated as **absent**, not as an error | `create X --due ""` → no `due_at` | `src/cli/commands/create.rs:1496` | (case to add: `create_empty_due`) |
| S4.64 | **`defer`'s own JSON payload prints the instant differently from the store.** The `defer_until` value inside the `{"deferred":[…]}` document is RFC 3339 with the numeric offset spelling `+00:00`, not `Z`, while the same instant written into `.beads/issues.jsonl` uses `Z` per S4.54. Both use the 0/3/6/9 fraction rule | `defer proj-mta --until 2027-06-01 --json` → stdout `…"defer_until":"2027-06-01T09:00:00+00:00"}]}` and the store line `…"defer_until":"2027-06-01T09:00:00Z",…` | `src/cli/commands/defer.rs:352` | defer_until_date, defer_relative |
| S4.79 | `defer` **without** `--until` parses no timestamp at all: the deferral is indefinite and the record carries no `defer_until`. `undefer` likewise parses nothing and clears any `defer_until` it finds. Re-deferring a record that is already deferred to the identical instant (including the identical absence) is a no-op | `defer proj-mta` → stdout `⏱ Deferred proj-mta: Set up database schema (indefinitely)`, no `defer_until` in the record; `undefer proj-7vm --json` → `{"undeferred":[{…,"previous_status":"deferred","status":"open"}]}` and the record loses `defer_until`; `undefer proj-mta` → stdout `⊘ Skipped proj-mta: not deferred (status: open)`, exit 0, nothing changes | `src/cli/commands/defer.rs:251`, `src/cli/commands/defer.rs:584` | defer_no_until, undefer_basic, undefer_not_deferred |

### Value parsing

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.65 | **Priority parsing.** The value is trimmed, then uppercased in full, then a single leading `P` is removed when present; the remainder is parsed as a signed 32-bit integer. A value in 0..=4 is the priority. Because the uppercase happens first, the lowercase forms `p2` and `P2` and the bare `2` are all accepted. A leading `+` is accepted by the integer parse. Interior or surrounding spaces other than the outer trim are rejected | `2` → 2; `P2` → 2; `p2` → 2; ` 3 ` → 3; `+2` → 2 | `src/model/mod.rs:177` | create_priority_p_form, list_priority_one, update_priority, update_noop_same_value, q_priority |
| S4.66 | A priority that parses but falls outside 0..=4 fails with `Priority must be 0-4, got: <n>` naming the parsed integer. A value that does not parse fails with the same message naming the value **after** trim, uppercase and `P`-stripping — so a lowercase input is reported uppercased. The hint is `Priority must be 0-4 or P0-P4 (0=critical, 4=backlog); list filters also accept ranges and comma lists such as 0-1 or 0,2.` (with the trailing full stop). Code `INVALID_PRIORITY`, exit **4**, retryable true; JSON context carries `priority` and `hint` | `-p 9` → `Priority must be 0-4, got: 9`; `-p 7` → `…got: 7`; `-p x` → `…got: X` (uppercased) | `src/error/mod.rs:107`, `src/error/structured.rs:539` | error_create_bad_priority, error_create_bad_priority_json, error_update_bad_priority; (case to add: `error_create_priority_word`) |
| S4.67 | **Priority filters** (`list`, `ready`, `blocked`, `count` `-p`/`--priority`, repeatable) parse a different grammar. Each occurrence is split on `,`; each token is trimmed; empty tokens are skipped. A token that splits at its **first** `-` into two parts that are both non-empty after trimming is an **inclusive range**: both sides parse per S4.65 and every priority from low through high is selected; a range running backwards fails with `Priority must be 0-4, got: <token>` naming the whole token. Any other token is a single priority per S4.65. The selected values are de-duplicated and returned in **ascending** order | `-p 1` → [1]; `-p 0-1` → [0,1]; `-p P0-P1` → [0,1]; `-p 0,2` → [0,2]; `-p 2 -p 0` → [0,2]; `-p 3-1` → error naming `3-1`; `-p -1` (first `-` splits into an empty left part) → falls through to the single-value path → error naming `-1` | `src/validation/mod.rs:38` | list_priority_one; (cases to add: `list_priority_range`, `list_priority_list`, `error_list_priority_backwards`) |
| S4.68 | `--priority-min` and `--priority-max` take a plain unsigned 8-bit integer through the argument parser, not the S4.65 grammar: `P1` is rejected by the parser with exit **2**, and a value above 255 is rejected by the parser | `--priority-min 1 --priority-max 3` → the inclusive band 1..=3 | `src/cli/mod.rs:1861` | list_priority_min_max |
| S4.69 | **`--estimate`/`-e`** takes a signed 32-bit integer through the argument parser. A negative value fails validation with `estimated_minutes: cannot be negative`; a value above **525960** fails with `estimated_minutes: exceeds maximum (525960 minutes / ~1 year)`. Both are exit **4**. A value that does not fit a signed 32-bit integer is rejected by the argument parser with exit **2** | `-e 90` → `"estimated_minutes":90`; `-e -1` → the negative message; `-e 525961` → the maximum message | `src/cli/mod.rs:1103`, `src/validation/mod.rs:112` | create_full_json; (cases to add: `error_create_negative_estimate`, `error_create_huge_estimate`) |
| S4.70 | **Label splitting.** `-l`/`--labels` is repeatable and each occurrence is additionally split on `,` by the argument parser. Each resulting label is then trimmed; an empty result is dropped; a label already collected is dropped (first occurrence wins, comparison is exact byte equality). `q` performs the same split-trim-drop itself | `-l one,two` → `["one","two"]`; `-l "  trimmed  "` → `["trimmed"]`; `-l a -l a` → `["a"]`; `-l ",,"` → none | `src/cli/mod.rs:1090`, `src/cli/commands/create.rs:772`, `src/cli/commands/q.rs:14` | create_full_json, list_label_two_and, update_set_labels |
| S4.71 | **Label validation.** A label must be non-empty, at most **50 bytes**, and made only of ASCII alphanumerics, `-`, `_` and `:`. Otherwise the run fails with `Validation failed: label: <reason>` on stderr, exit **4**, where the reason is `cannot be empty`, `exceeds 50 characters` or `invalid characters (only alphanumeric, hyphen, underscore, colon allowed)`. A record may carry at most **64** labels (`labels: exceeds 64 labels`) | `-l "bad label!"` → `Error: Validation failed: label: invalid characters (only alphanumeric, hyphen, underscore, colon allowed)`, exit 4; `backend`, `a-b_c:d`, `x1` → valid | `src/validation/mod.rs:428`, `src/validation/mod.rs:24` | error_create_bad_label, error_label_add_invalid |
| S4.72 | **Status spellings.** A status value is lowercased and matched against `open`, `in_progress` (the spelling `inprogress` is also accepted and folds to `in_progress`), `blocked`, `deferred`, `draft`, `closed`, `tombstone`, `pinned`. Anything else becomes a **custom** status carrying the lowercased text. On `create -s` and `update --status` a custom value is accepted; on the `list --status` filter an undeclared custom value is refused with `Validation failed: status: unknown status '<value>'. Built-in statuses: open, in_progress, blocked, deferred, draft, closed, tombstone, pinned. Custom statuses must be declared in .beads/policy.yaml (workflow.statuses) or exist on at least one issue. Use --status all to include every status`, exit **4**. A custom status is capped at 50 characters | `-s in_progress` → `in_progress`; `-s InProgress` → `in_progress`; `--status bogus` on `list` → the refusal above | `src/model/mod.rs:87`, `src/model/mod.rs:139` | create_status_in_progress, list_status_in_progress, list_status_bad |
| S4.73 | **Issue-type spellings.** A type value is lowercased and matched against `task`, `bug`, `feature`, `epic`, `chore`, `docs`, `question`. Anything else becomes a **custom** type carrying the lowercased text and is accepted on create; a custom type is capped at 50 characters | `-t feature` → `feature`; `-t spike` → `spike`; `-t SPIKE` → `spike` | `src/model/mod.rs:220`, `src/model/mod.rs:261` | create_custom_type, create_full_json, list_type_bug |
| S4.74 | **Dependency-type spellings.** A dependency type is lowercased and matched against `blocks`, `parent-child`, `conditional-blocks`, `waits-for`, `related`, `discovered-from`, `replies-to`, `relates-to`, `duplicates`, `supersedes`, `caused-by`. Anything else becomes a custom type carrying the lowercased text | `-t related` → `related`; `-t inspired-by` → `inspired-by` | `src/model/mod.rs:288` | dep_add_related, dep_add_custom_type, dep_add_blocks |
| S4.75 | **Title limits.** A title that is empty or whitespace-only fails with `Validation failed: title: cannot be empty`, exit **4**. A title longer than **500 characters** — counted in Unicode scalar values, not bytes, unlike the id seed of S4.1 — fails with `Validation failed: title: exceeds 500 characters`, exit **4**. Exactly 500 is accepted. A title containing a NUL byte fails with `title: contains NUL byte` | `""` → cannot be empty; `"   "` → cannot be empty; 500 `t` characters → accepted (`edge_create_max_title` → `proj-bc6`, **✓recomputed**); 501 `t` characters → exceeds 500 characters | `src/validation/mod.rs:162`, `src/validation/mod.rs:21` | error_create_empty_title, error_create_blank_title, error_create_long_title, edge_create_max_title |
| S4.76 | **Comment text.** A comment whose text is empty fails with `Validation failed: text: comment text cannot be empty`, exit **4** | `comments add proj-mta ""` → that message | `src/validation/mod.rs:460` | error_comments_add_empty |
| S4.77 | **`--limit` and `--offset`** are unsigned pointer-width integers (64-bit on the pinned target). `--limit 0` means **unlimited**, and 0 is also the value printed in the JSON envelope when the user passed no limit. `has_more` is false whenever the effective limit is 0; otherwise it is true exactly when the total is strictly greater than offset plus limit, that addition being saturating | no `--limit` → `"total":7,"limit":0,"offset":0,"has_more":false`; `--limit 1` → `"total":7,"limit":1,"offset":0,"has_more":true`; `--limit 2 --offset 1` → `"total":7,"limit":2,"offset":1,"has_more":true` | `src/cli/mod.rs:1886`, `src/cli/commands/list.rs:221` | list_json, list_limit_one, list_limit_offset, empty_list_json, scn_dup_title |
| S4.78 | A `--limit` value that is not a non-negative decimal integer is refused by the **argument parser** before any workspace is touched: stderr carries `error: invalid value 'x' for '--limit <LIMIT>': invalid digit found in string`, a blank line, then `For more information, try '--help'.`, exit **2**. A `--limit` with no value carries `error: a value is required for '--limit <LIMIT>' but none was supplied` in the same shape | `list --limit x` → exit 2 as above; `list --limit` → exit 2 as above | `src/cli/mod.rs:1886` | usage_limit_bad, usage_limit_noval |


<!-- from docs/spec-parts/S4b.md -->

### Shared query vocabulary

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.100 | **Default visibility.** Every issue-listing relation (`list`, `search`, `count`, `epic status`) selects from the issue set minus a status exclusion. With `include_closed` false and `include_deferred` true the excluded statuses are exactly `closed` and `tombstone`. With `include_closed` false and `include_deferred` false the excluded statuses are `closed`, `tombstone` and `deferred`. With `include_closed` true and no explicit `--status`, the only excluded status is `tombstone`. With `include_closed` true and an explicit `--status`, nothing is excluded by this rule (a `--status tombstone` request therefore shows tombstones). Status matching is on the stored lowercase status string; custom statuses are never excluded by this rule. | fixture `basic`, 8 records, one `closed` (`proj-mkh`), one `deferred` (`proj-7vm`) → `list` yields 7 rows; `list --all` yields 8; `count` yields 6 | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8413`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:9141` | list_plain, list_all, list_all_json, count_plain, count_include_closed, list_graph |
| S4.101 | **`include_closed` derivation for `list`/`search`.** `include_closed` is true when `--all`/`-a` is given, or when any `--status` value is the meta-value `all` (case-insensitive after trimming), or when any parsed `--status` value is terminal (`closed` or `tombstone`). Otherwise false. | `list --status closed --json` → the closed record is returned although `--all` was not given, `total` 1 | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:530`, `src/cli/commands/search.rs:597` | list_status_closed, search_status |
| S4.102 | **`include_deferred` derivation for `list`/`search`.** `include_deferred` is true when `--deferred` is given, or `--all` is given, or no `--status` was given at all, or a parsed `--status` value is `deferred`. It is false only when an explicit `--status` list was given that does not contain `deferred`. Deferred work is therefore visible by default and vanishes the moment any other status is named. | `list` → `proj-7vm` (deferred) present; `list --status open --json` → `total` 5, `proj-7vm` absent | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:537` | list_plain, list_status_open, list_deferred |
| S4.103 | **Templates are always excluded** from `list`, `search`, `count`, `ready`, the blocked relation and the epic child counts, unless `--include-templates` was given (`count` only). A record with no template marker counts as not a template. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-047): hidden from `list`, `ready` and `count`; `count --include-templates` counts it. | (no template in any fixture; every case exercises the "not a template" branch) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8426`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:10275` | list_plain *(case to add: `list_template_hidden` — a fixture with `"is_template":true`)*, list_template_hidden, ready_template_hidden, count_templates, count_include_templates |
| S4.104 | **`--status all`.** When any `--status` value equals `all` (ASCII case-insensitive, surrounding whitespace trimmed) the status filter is dropped entirely and both `include_closed` and `include_deferred` become true. It is not a status name and is never matched against a record. Corrected (2026-09-23, OQ-039): `--status all` does NOT list tombstones; the example's "plus tombstones" is wrong (`scn_list_status_all`: the deleted `proj-b75` is absent from the JSON and the plain form). | `list --status all` → same rows as `list --all` plus tombstones | `src/cli/commands/mod.rs:287` | scn_list_status_all |
| S4.105 | **Unknown `--status` value (error).** A `--status` value that is not one of `open`, `in_progress`/`inprogress`, `blocked`, `deferred`, `draft`, `closed`, `tombstone`, `pinned`, `all` (all matched after lowercasing) is a *custom* status. A custom status is accepted only when it appears in `.beads/policy.yaml` `workflow.statuses` or is the status of at least one stored issue (both compared lowercased); otherwise the run fails before any query with exit code `4` and this single stderr line (with a trailing newline, no trailing space): `Error: Validation failed: status: unknown status 'bogus'. Built-in statuses: open, in_progress, blocked, deferred, draft, closed, tombstone, pinned. Custom statuses must be declared in .beads/policy.yaml (workflow.statuses) or exist on at least one issue. Use --status all to include every status` — the quoted name is the user's value verbatim, not lowercased. stdout is empty. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-114): the JSON envelope has `hint` null, `retryable` true, `context` `{"field":"status","reason":…}` (`list_status_bad_json`). | `list --status bogus` → exit 4, the line above | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:449` | list_status_bad, list_status_bad_json |
| S4.106 | **Custom status accepted.** A `--status` value that is not built-in but *is* carried by a stored issue passes S4.105 and then matches records whose stored status equals the value lowercased. It is never terminal, so it does not set `include_closed`. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-040): a stored custom `Triage` loads as `triage` and is selected by `--status Triage` and `--status triage`; plain mode shows the `?` glyph. | *(case to add: `list_status_custom` on a fixture whose record carries `"status":"triage"`)* | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:459`, `src/model/mod.rs:139` | *(case to add as above)*, list_status_custom, list_status_custom_lower |
| S4.107 | **`--priority`/`-p` forms (list, count, blocked, ready).** Each occurrence is split on `,`; each token is trimmed and an empty token is skipped. A token containing `-` with a non-empty part on each side is a closed range: both ends are parsed as priorities and every integer from low to high inclusive is selected; low greater than high is an error. Any other token is one priority. A priority token is trimmed, uppercased, an optional leading `P` is stripped, and the remainder must parse as an integer in `0..=4`. The selected set is deduplicated and yielded in ascending numeric order. The filter then matches records whose priority is in that set. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-037, OQ-116): `3-1` is `Priority must be 0-4, got: 3-1` with the hint `Ranges run low to high: use 1-3.`; `P9` quotes `9` with the long hint (`list_priority_backwards`, `list_priority_p9`), exit 4. | `-p 1` → `{1}` → `proj-170`, `proj-mta`; `-p 0-1` → `{0,1}` → `proj-ptp`, `proj-170`, `proj-mta`; `-p P3,0` → `{0,3}` | `src/validation/mod.rs:38`, `src/model/mod.rs:174` | list_priority_one, *(case to add: `list_priority_range` — `["@fx=basic","list","-p","0-1"]`, observed: `proj-ptp`, `proj-170`, `proj-mta`)*, list_priority_backwards, list_priority_p9 |
| S4.108 | **`--priority-min` / `--priority-max` bounds (error).** Both are parsed by the argument layer as unsigned integers, then validated to lie in `0..=4`. Out of range fails with exit code `4` and stderr `Error: Priority must be 0-4, got: 5` followed by `Hint: Priority must be 0-4 or P0-P4 (0=critical, 4=backlog); list filters also accept ranges and comma lists such as 0-1 or 0,2.` (each line newline-terminated). No comparison between min and max is performed, so `--priority-min 4 --priority-max 0` is accepted and yields no rows. | `list --priority-min 5` → exit 4, the two lines above | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:867` | list_priority_min_max, *(case to add: `error_list_priority_min_bad` — `["@fx=basic","list","--priority-min","5"]`)* |
| S4.109 | **`--title-contains` matching (`LIKE`).** The needle is escaped by replacing `\` with `\\`, then `%` with `\%`, then `_` with `\_`, and wrapped as `%<escaped>%`; the comparison is SQL `LIKE … ESCAPE '\'`, which folds case for the 26 ASCII letters only. A non-ASCII letter therefore matches case-sensitively, and `%`/`_` in the needle are literal. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-048): `--title-contains ärger` and `search ärger` do not find `Ärger`: ASCII-only. | `--title-contains auth` → `proj-170` ("Implement user **auth**"), `proj-uly` ("**Auth** epic"); `--title-contains Ünï` → `proj-b75`; `--title-contains ünï` → no rows; `--title-contains %` → no rows | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8434`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:18814` | list_title_contains, *(case to add: `list_title_contains_unicode_lower` — `["@fx=basic","list","--title-contains","ünï"]` → empty; `list_title_contains_percent` — `["@fx=basic","list","--title-contains","%"]` → empty)*, list_title_contains_nonascii, search_nonascii |
| S4.110 | **Timestamp comparison.** `created_at` / `updated_at` comparisons are chronological on the instant, not textual on the input bytes. Two inputs denoting the same instant compare equal however they were spelled. Mechanism: each timestamp is parsed on load and re-emitted in one canonical form (UTC, fixed `+00:00` offset, fractional digits 0, 3, 6 or 9 — the shortest that is exact), and lexicographic order of that canonical form coincides with chronological order; the Rust-side default sort compares parsed instants directly. [inference: that the two paths coincide — both were observed to give the same order on fixture `precision`.] | fixture `precision`: `…05Z`, `…05.000000001Z`, `…05.100Z`, `…05.1Z`, `12:00:04+02:00` order ascending as `aae` (10:00:04Z) < `aaa` (…05) < `aac` (…05.000000001) < `aab` = `aad` (…05.100) < `aaf` (…06) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:1658`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:18248`; resolves OQ-001 | edge_precision_ready, edge_precision_sort_created, edge_precision_list_json |
| S4.111 | **Id tie-break.** Every ordering in this section ends with the issue id ascending, compared as a byte sequence (no case folding, no numeric awareness). Ids are unique, so every ordering below is total and the sorts' stability never reaches an output. | `proj-aab` before `proj-aad` at the identical instant `…05.100Z`; `proj-170` before `proj-5u2` at the identical `updated_at` `11:00:00Z` (`'1'` 0x31 < `'5'` 0x35) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:18253`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8467` | edge_precision_ready, list_sort_updated |
| S4.112 | **Title ordering (`COLLATE NOCASE`).** `--sort title` orders by title with the ASCII-only case-insensitive collation: bytes `A`–`Z` (0x41–0x5A) compare equal to `a`–`z` (0x61–0x7A); every other byte compares by its own value, so any byte of a multi-byte UTF-8 character (≥ 0xC2) sorts after every ASCII character. | `precision` fixture ascending: `apple lower title`, `empty strings kept`, `millis`, `nanos`, `offset zone`, `one tenth short form`, `UPPER case title`, `whole second`; `basic` ascending ends with `Ünïcödé title …` after `Write API docs` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8460` | edge_precision_sort_title, list_sort_title |
| S4.113 | **The pinned instant.** Every "now" in this section (`ready`'s `defer_until` comparison, `stats`' ready count, `list --overdue`) is one wall-clock read per invocation. Under the capture harness it is the case's `@time=` value, default `2026-01-02 03:04:05` UTC. `defer_until` and `due_at` comparisons are against that instant, both sides as instants. | `proj-7vm` has `defer_until` `2027-06-01T09:00:00Z` > now → excluded from `ready` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:10262`, `src/cli/commands/stats.rs:513` | ready_plain, ready_include_deferred, scn_lifecycle, q_fraction, update_fraction, close_fraction_json |

### `list`

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.114 | **The `list` relation.** `list` selects every issue that survives S4.100/S4.103 and every filter in S4.121–S4.132 simultaneously (all filters conjoined), orders it by S4.115, then drops `--offset` rows and keeps at most `--limit` rows. Nothing is deduplicated; each issue appears at most once. | `@fx=basic list` → 7 rows in the order `proj-ptp`, `proj-170`, `proj-mta`, `proj-b75`, `proj-uly`, `proj-5u2`, `proj-7vm` | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:62`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8334` | empty_list, list_plain, list_json, list_json_again |
| S4.115 | **Default order (`priority ASC, created_at DESC, id ASC`).** With no `--sort` and no `--reverse`, rows are ordered by priority ascending (0 first), then `created_at` descending (newest first, S4.110), then id ascending (S4.111). This order is produced by three code paths that agree: a Rust sort over parsed values when limit is 0/absent and offset is 0; a per-priority scan 0,1,2,3,4 each ordered `created_at DESC, id ASC` when a positive limit is given with offset 0 and no other filter; and SQL `ORDER BY priority ASC, created_at DESC, id ASC` otherwise. | `basic`: P0 `proj-ptp`; P1 `proj-170` (10:00:01) then `proj-mta` (10:00:00); P2 `proj-b75` (10:00:06) then `proj-uly` (10:00:04); P3 `proj-5u2`; P4 `proj-7vm`. With `--all`, P2 becomes `proj-mkh` (10:00:07), `proj-b75`, `proj-uly` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:18248`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8519`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8484` | list_plain, list_all, list_limit_one, list_limit_offset |
| S4.116 | **`--sort` keys.** Accepted keys and their orders (ascending direction shown; `--reverse` flips as stated in S4.117): `priority` → priority ascending, `created_at` descending, id ascending; `created_at` and its alias `created` → `created_at` descending, id ascending; `updated_at` and its alias `updated` → `updated_at` descending, id ascending; `title` → title ascending under S4.112, id ascending. Note that `created_at`/`updated_at` are *descending* in the un-reversed form. | `--sort created_at` on `basic` → `proj-b75`(06), `proj-7vm`(05), `proj-uly`(04), `proj-5u2`(03), `proj-ptp`(02), `proj-170`(01), `proj-mta`(00); `--sort updated_at` → `proj-7vm`(14:00), `proj-ptp`(12:30), `proj-170`(11:00), `proj-5u2`(11:00), `proj-b75`, `proj-uly`, `proj-mta` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8444` | list_sort_created, list_sort_updated, list_sort_title, edge_precision_sort_created, edge_precision_sort_title |
| S4.117 | **`--reverse`/`-r`.** With no `--sort`, `--reverse` gives `priority DESC, created_at ASC, id ASC` — the id tie-break stays ascending. With `--sort priority` it gives `priority DESC, created_at ASC, id ASC`; with `--sort created_at`/`updated_at` it flips that key to ascending; with `--sort title` it flips the title key to descending. In every case the trailing id key remains ascending. | `list -r` on `basic` → `proj-7vm`, `proj-5u2`, `proj-uly`(10:00:04), `proj-b75`(10:00:06), `proj-mta`(10:00:00), `proj-170`(10:00:01), `proj-ptp` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8487` | list_reverse |
| S4.118 | **Bad `--sort` (error).** A `--sort` value other than `priority`, `created_at`, `updated_at`, `title`, `created`, `updated` fails before the query with exit code `4` and the single stderr line `Error: Validation failed: sort: invalid sort field 'nonsense'` (newline-terminated, the value quoted verbatim). stdout is empty. For `list` the check runs *after* the status check of S4.105 and after the query filters are built; for `search` it runs first. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-114): the JSON envelope has `hint` null, `retryable` true, `context` `{"field":"sort","reason":…}` (`list_sort_bad_json`). | `list --sort nonsense` → exit 4, that line | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:853`, `src/cli/commands/search.rs:656` | list_sort_bad, list_sort_bad_json |
| S4.119 | **`--limit` and `--offset`.** `list`'s `--limit` default is **0**, and 0 means unlimited — the whole matching set. `--offset` default is 0. A positive limit keeps the first `limit` rows after skipping `offset` rows of the ordered relation. An offset at or past the end yields no rows (not an error). | `list --limit 1 --json` → 1 row (`proj-ptp`); `list --limit 2 --offset 1 --json` → `proj-170`, `proj-mta`; `list --offset 99 --json` → `"issues":[]` with `"total":7` | `src/cli/mod.rs:30`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8492` | list_limit_one, list_limit_offset, *(case to add: `list_offset_past_end` — `["@fx=basic","list","--offset","99","--json"]`)* |
| S4.120 | **The structured page envelope `{issues,total,limit,offset,has_more}`.** `limit` and `offset` echo the user's values (0 and 0 by default). `total` is the count of the matching set *before* limit and offset: it is a separate count query when structured output is requested and (`limit` ≠ 0 or `offset` ≠ 0) and no client-side filter is active; otherwise it is the length of the returned row vector. `has_more` is `false` when `limit` is 0, else `total > offset + limit` (saturating addition). | `list --json` → `"total":7,"limit":0,"offset":0,"has_more":false`; `list --limit 1 --json` → `"total":7,"limit":1,"has_more":true`; `list --limit 2 --offset 1 --json` → `"total":7,"limit":2,"offset":1,"has_more":true`; `list --offset 6 --limit 2 --json` → 1 row, `"total":7,"has_more":false`; empty store → `"total":0,"limit":0,"offset":0,"has_more":false` | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:101`, `src/cli/commands/list.rs:216` | empty_list_json, list_json, list_limit_one, list_limit_offset, list_all_json |
| S4.121 | **Truncation notes (text output only).** When text output was truncated by a positive `--limit` the run writes one stderr line and continues with exit 0. On the SQL path (no client-side filter) the line is `[note] Output truncated to 2 issues. Use --limit 0 for all results.`; on the client-filter path (S4.133) the exact pre-truncation total is known and the line is `[note] Showing 2 of 7 issues. Use --limit 0 for all results.`. Neither line is emitted for `--json`/`--toon`, nor under `--quiet`. | `list --limit 2` → two rows on stdout plus the first line on stderr | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:188` | *(case to add: `list_limit_note` — `["@fx=basic","list","--limit","2"]`)* |
| S4.122 | **`--status`/`-s` filter.** Repeatable; the record's status must equal one of the values, each lowercased (S4.105/S4.106). Several values are an OR. The status filter also feeds S4.101/S4.102, so naming `closed` makes closed rows visible and naming only non-deferred statuses hides deferred rows. | `list -s in_progress` → `proj-ptp`; `list -s open -s closed --json` → 6 rows, `"total":6`, ordered `proj-170`, `proj-mta`, `proj-mkh`, `proj-b75`, `proj-uly`, `proj-5u2` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8375` | list_status_open, list_status_closed, list_status_in_progress, *(case to add: `list_status_two` — `["@fx=basic","list","-s","open","-s","closed","--json"]`)* |
| S4.123 | **`--type`/`-t` filter.** Repeatable; the record's issue type must equal one of the parsed values. Built-in types are matched case-insensitively and an unrecognized name becomes a custom type matched by its lowercased spelling. Several values are an OR. | `list -t bug --json` → `proj-ptp` only, `"total":1` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8387` | list_type_bug |
| S4.124 | **`--assignee` and `--unassigned`.** `--assignee <v>` keeps records whose stored assignee equals `v` exactly (byte equality, case-sensitive); a record with no assignee never matches. `--unassigned` keeps records whose assignee is absent or the empty string. The two flags are mutually exclusive at the argument layer. | `list --assignee alice --json` → `proj-170` only; `list --unassigned` → `proj-mta`, `proj-b75`, `proj-uly`, `proj-5u2`, `proj-7vm` (the `alice` and `bob` rows dropped) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:8407` | list_assignee, list_unassigned |
| S4.125 | **`--id` filter.** Repeatable. Applied in Rust after the SQL query, it keeps records whose id is *exactly* one of the given strings — no prefix, hash or partial-id resolution (unlike `show`). An id that matches nothing simply contributes nothing. | `list --id proj-mta --json` → 1 row, `"total":1` | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:640` | list_id_filter |
| S4.126 | **`--label`/`-l` (AND).** Repeatable. Duplicate values are collapsed first. One distinct label keeps records carrying that label. Two or more distinct labels keep only records carrying *all* of them. Label comparison is exact byte equality. | `-l backend --json` → `proj-170`, `proj-mta`; `-l backend -l auth` → `proj-170` only | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:1019` | list_label, list_label_two_and |
| S4.127 | **`--label-any` (OR).** Repeatable, not deduplicated. Keeps records carrying at least one of the values. | `--label-any db --label-any auth` → `proj-170` (auth), `proj-mta` (db) | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:1056` | list_label_any |
| S4.128 | **`--label` and `--label-any` together** are conjoined: a record must carry every `--label` value *and* at least one `--label-any` value. | *(case to add: `list_label_and_any` — `["@fx=basic","list","-l","backend","--label-any","db"]` → `proj-mta`)* | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:1019` | *(case to add as above)* |
| S4.129 | **`--desc-contains` and `--notes-contains`.** Applied in Rust after the SQL query. The needle is lowercased with Rust's full Unicode lowercasing and the haystack (the record's `description` / `notes`, or the empty string when absent) is lowercased the same way; the test is substring containment. Because the folding is Unicode here and ASCII-only in `--title-contains` (S4.109) and `search` (S4.137), these two options fold non-ASCII case while the others do not. Measured (2026-09-23): `--desc-contains "LINE ONE"` matches the description `Line one…` (`list_desc_contains_unicode`); the non-ASCII fold stays OQ-048. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-048, OQ-117): `école` finds `ÉCOLE`. The port folds ASCII, Latin-1, Latin Extended-A and Additional, Greek and Cyrillic. | `--desc-contains tables` → `proj-mta` ("Create the initial **tables**."); `--notes-contains z` → no rows (no fixture record has notes) | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:652` | *(case to add: `list_desc_contains` — `["@fx=basic","list","--desc-contains","tables"]`; `list_notes_contains_none` — `["@fx=basic","list","--notes-contains","z"]`)*, list_desc_contains_unicode, list_desc_contains_nonascii |
| S4.130 | **`--priority-min` / `--priority-max`.** Applied in Rust after the SQL query, after the bounds check of S4.108: a record is kept when its priority is ≥ min (when given) and ≤ max (when given). | `--priority-min 1 --priority-max 3 --json` → `proj-170`, `proj-mta`, `proj-b75`, `proj-uly`, `proj-5u2`; `"total":5` | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:664` | list_priority_min_max |
| S4.131 | **`--deferred`.** It does *not* restrict the result to deferred issues; it forces `include_deferred` true (S4.102) and switches `list` onto the client-filter path (S4.133). With no other filter its output is byte-identical to plain `list`, because deferred issues are already visible by default. | `list --deferred` → the same 7 rows in the same order as `list` | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:583`, `src/cli/commands/list.rs:684` | list_deferred, list_plain |
| S4.132 | **`--overdue`.** Applied in Rust: a record is kept only when it has a `due_at`, that instant is strictly before the pinned now (S4.113), and its status is not terminal (`closed`/`tombstone`). `--overdue` additionally flips the deferred default: with `--overdue` and no `--status` and no `--deferred`/`--all`, deferred records are hidden. When nothing is overdue the command prints nothing and exits 0. Measured on fixture `due` at 2026-06-01 (2026-09-22): `--all` and `--deferred` bring the deferred past-due record back and the closed one never shows; `--status deferred` alone keeps only it; a due date given as a bare day is stored at `09:00:00Z` of that day (`list_overdue_json`). | `list --overdue` on `basic` (no record has `due_at`) → empty stdout, exit 0 | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:687` | *(case to add: `list_overdue_none` — `["@fx=basic","list","--overdue"]` → empty stdout, exit 0)*, list_overdue, list_overdue_json, list_overdue_all, list_overdue_status_deferred, list_overdue_deferred, list_overdue_priority, list_overdue_none |
| S4.133 | **How filters combine and in what order.** All filters are conjunctive. `--status`, `--type`, `--priority`, `--assignee`, `--unassigned`, `--label`, `--label-any`, `--title-contains` and the visibility rule run inside the query; `--id`, `--priority-min`, `--priority-max`, `--desc-contains`, `--notes-contains`, `--deferred` and `--overdue` run afterwards in Rust. When any of the latter seven is present the query runs *without* `LIMIT`/`OFFSET` and the Rust stage applies the offset (by dropping the leading rows) and then the limit; `total` then equals the post-filter, pre-offset row count. The result order is unchanged by the Rust stage. | `list --limit 2 --id proj-mta --id proj-170` → `proj-170`, `proj-mta` (query order kept), no truncation note | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:113`, `src/cli/commands/list.rs:160` | list_id_filter, list_priority_min_max |
| S4.134 | **`--fields` does not change selection.** `--fields` names CSV columns only. In text and JSON modes the option is parsed and then ignored: the row set, the row order and every emitted field are exactly those of the same invocation without it. | `list --json --fields id,title,status,priority` → byte-identical to `list --json` | `src/cli/mod.rs:1942`, `src/cli/commands/list.rs:206` | list_fields, list_json |
| S4.135 | **`--quiet`.** Every filter, sort and count still runs; all stdout and the stderr notes of S4.121 are suppressed and the exit code is unchanged. Errors (S4.105, S4.108, S4.118) are still reported. | `list --quiet` → empty stdout, empty stderr, exit 0 | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:195` | list_quiet |
| S4.136 | **`--long` selects the same rows.** `list --long` changes only the rendering (S5); the relation, filters and order are those of S4.114/S4.115. | `list --long` → the same seven ids in the same order as `list` | `src/cli/commands/list.rs:139` | list_long, list_plain |

### `search`

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.137 | **The `search` relation — fields and matching rule.** The query string is trimmed; an all-whitespace or empty query is the error of S4.142. A record matches when the needle occurs as a **substring** (not tokenized, no word boundaries, no ranking) in any one of four places: its `title`, its `description`, its `id`, or the `text` of any of its comments. Comparison is on both sides lowercased by SQL `lower()` / Rust `to_ascii_lowercase()`, which fold the 26 ASCII letters and leave every other byte alone. An absent `description` never matches. | `search auth` → `proj-170`, `proj-uly`; `search AUTH` → the same two; `search tables` → `proj-mta` (description); `search PROJ-MTA` → `proj-mta` (id); `search staging` → `proj-ptp` (comment "Reproduced on staging."); `search zzzz` → none Amended (2026-09-24, bb-rvt): the rule holds for a field of any length (`search_long_texts`: an 80 KB description and 40 KB of notes). | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:1582`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:9709` | search_plain, search_case_insensitive, search_description, search_none, empty_search, search_long_texts |
| S4.138 | **Non-ASCII case in `search`.** Because the folding of S4.137 is ASCII-only, a needle whose letters are non-ASCII matches case-sensitively. | `search Ünï` → `proj-b75`; `search ünï` → `Found 0 issue(s) matching 'ünï'` | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:9711` | search_unicode, *(case to add: `search_unicode_lower` — `["@fx=basic","search","ünï"]` → 0 results)* |
| S4.139 | **`search` order.** There is no relevance ranking: matched rows are ordered exactly as `list` is — default `priority ASC, created_at DESC, id ASC`, or the `--sort`/`--reverse` order of S4.116/S4.117. | `search auth` → `proj-170` (P1) then `proj-uly` (P2); `search auth --sort title` → `proj-uly` ("Auth epic") then `proj-170` ("Implement user auth") | `src/storage/sqlite.rs:9718` | search_plain, *(case to add: `search_sort_title` — `["@fx=basic","search","auth","--sort","title"]`)* |
| S4.140 | **`search` accepts every `list` filter** and conjoins them with the match of S4.137, with the same visibility derivation (S4.101/S4.102) and the same client-filter split (S4.133) — except that `search`'s `--priority` parses each value as a *single* priority with no ranges and no comma lists (S4.148 records the divergence). | `search work --status closed` → `proj-mkh`; `search auth --id proj-uly` → `proj-uly`; `search auth -p 1` → `proj-170` | `src/cli/commands/search.rs:559` | search_status, *(case to add: `search_id_filter` — `["@fx=basic","search","auth","--id","proj-uly"]`)* |
| S4.141 | **`search` pagination.** `search`'s `--limit` default is **50** (not 0); 0 means unlimited. The query fetches `limit + 1` rows; `has_more` is true when more than `limit` came back, and the extra row is dropped. The JSON envelope is `{issues, hidden_closed_count, limit, offset, has_more}` — it has **no** `total` field. In text mode the header word changes with `has_more`: `Found N issue(s) matching 'q'` when false, `Showing N issue(s) matching 'q'` when true, and a trailing **stdout** line `note: showing 1 search result(s) from offset 0; more matches exist. Use --limit 0 for all results (current limit: 1)` is printed when true. | `search auth --json` → `"limit":50,"offset":0,"has_more":false`; `search auth --limit 1 --json` → 1 row, `"limit":1,"has_more":true`; `search auth --limit 1` → the `Showing …` header plus the note line | `src/cli/mod.rs:39`, `src/cli/commands/search.rs:101`, `src/cli/commands/search.rs:353` | search_json, *(case to add: `search_limit_one` — `["@fx=basic","search","auth","--limit","1","--json"]`)* |
| S4.142 | **`hidden_closed_count`.** When the corpus excludes closed issues (`include_closed` false) and `--overdue` was not given, a second count runs over exactly the closed matches the exclusion hid, with the same needle and the same other filters and no limit/offset. It is reported as `hidden_closed_count` in JSON and, when greater than 0, as a trailing stdout line in text mode: `note: 1 closed match(es) hidden; rerun with --all to include them` — printed even when the visible result set is empty. With `include_closed` true, or under `--overdue`, it is 0 without a query. Tombstones are never counted. | `search work` → `Found 0 issue(s) matching 'work'` then the note line; `search work --json` → `{"issues":[],"hidden_closed_count":1,…}`; `search auth --json` → `"hidden_closed_count":0` | `src/cli/commands/search.rs:389`, `src/cli/commands/search.rs:376` | search_json, *(case to add: `search_hidden_closed` — `["@fx=basic","search","work"]` and `…,"--json"]`)* |
| S4.143 | **Empty `search` query (error).** A query that is empty or only whitespace fails before any query with exit code `4` and stderr `Error: Validation failed: query: search query cannot be empty`. | *(case to add: `error_search_empty_query` — `["@fx=basic","search","   "]`)* | `src/cli/commands/search.rs:81` | usage_search_alone *(the no-argument form is the argument-layer error, a different message: S1)* |

### `count`

| S4.n | clause | examples (input → output) | provenance | cases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| S4.144 | **`count`'s default corpus is narrower than `list`'s.** `count` starts from an empty filter set in which `include_deferred` is **false**, so by default it excludes `closed`, `tombstone` **and** `deferred`. `list` includes deferred by default (S4.102). `count` sets `include_deferred` true only when `--status all` is given or a `--status` value is `deferred`. | `@fx=basic count` → `6`; `@fx=basic list` → 7 rows | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:61`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:9141` | count_plain, count_json, empty_count |
| S4.145 | **`count --include-closed`.** Sets `include_closed` true; with no explicit `--status` the only remaining exclusion is `tombstone`, so deferred rows come back too. | `count --include-closed` → `8` (all of `basic`) | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:88` | count_include_closed |
| S4.146 | **`count` filters.** `--status` (comma-splittable, repeatable), `--type`, `--priority` (full form of S4.107), `--assignee`, `--unassigned`, `--include-closed`, `--include-templates`, `--title-contains`. They are conjoined exactly as in `list`. `count` has no `--id`, `--label`, `--limit`, `--offset`, `--sort`. A `--status` value setting `is_terminal` also sets `include_closed`. | `count --status closed` → 1 | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:60` | count_plain *(case to add: `count_status_closed` — `["@fx=basic","count","--status","closed"]`)* |
| S4.147 | **Grouping selectors.** `--by <field>` or one of the aliases `--by-status`, `--by-priority`, `--by-type`, `--by-assignee`, `--by-label`. Selecting more than one is an error: exit code `4`, stderr `Error: Validation failed: by: only one count grouping selector can be specified; got --by-status, --by-type` — the flags are listed in the fixed order `--by`, `--by-status`, `--by-priority`, `--by-type`, `--by-assignee`, `--by-label`, joined with `, `. Measured (2026-09-23, OQ-114): two grouping selectors are `Validation failed: by: only one count grouping selector can be specified; got --by-status, --by-type`, exit 4; the JSON context is `{"field":"by","reason":…}` (`count_by_two`, `count_by_two_json`). | `count --by-status --by-type` → exit 4, that line | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:140` | *(case to add: `error_count_two_groupings` — `["@fx=basic","count","--by-status","--by-type"]`)*, count_by_two, count_by_two_json |
| S4.148 | **Group keys and the missing-value labels.** For `status` the key is the stored status string; for `priority` it is `P` followed by the digit (`P0`…`P4`); for `type` it is the type string; for `assignee` it is the assignee, or the literal `(unassigned)` when absent; for `label` one entry per label the issue carries, or the literal `(no labels)` when the issue carries none. Each issue contributes 1 to exactly one group except under `--by-label`, where a multi-label issue contributes 1 to each of its labels — so the group counts then sum to more than `total`. | `count --by-assignee` → `(unassigned): 4`, `alice: 1`, `bob: 1`, total 6; `count --by-label` → `(no labels): 4`, `auth: 1`, `backend: 2`, `db: 1`, total 6 (sum 8) | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:266`, `src/storage/sqlite.rs:9341` | count_by_status, count_by_priority_json, count_by_type, count_by_assignee, count_by_label |
| S4.149 | **Group order.** Groups are emitted in ascending byte order of the key string — not by count, not by priority number. `(` is 0x28 and therefore sorts before every ASCII letter, so `(unassigned)` and `(no labels)` come first; `P0`…`P4` happen to sort numerically because they are equal-length. | `count --by-type` → `bug`, `docs`, `epic`, `feature`, `task`; `count --by-priority --json` → `[{"group":"P0",…},{"group":"P1",…},{"group":"P2",…},{"group":"P3",…}]` | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:271` | count_by_type, count_by_assignee, count_by_label, count_by_priority_json |
| S4.150 | **`total` under grouping** is the number of *issues* in the filtered corpus, computed independently of the group counts (so it is unaffected by the multi-label inflation of S4.148). Groups with count 0 are never emitted. | `count --by-label` → `Total: 6` with four groups summing to 8 | `src/cli/commands/count.rs:328` | count_by_label, count_by_status |
